 [vote?id=50548510&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50548510]
black3r [user?id=black3r] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548510] | next [#50550277] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The bug was apparently fixed in open source repo a week earlier, this could've been abused by anyone smart enough to monitor repos like this for exploitable bugfixes for a week (the commit message even included "Reported by Xint.io" and I'm guessing skilled black hats would know about Xint)
Given that in this case the workaround is as simple as disabling http2, there is honestly no reason why F5/Nginx or any of the reporting teams couldn't at least put out a PSA to disable http2 for the time being before basically telling the whole wide world this exists and how to exploit it.
Especially for software like Nginx which powers a huge part of the internet.

 [vote?id=50550971&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50550971]
weitendorf [user?id=weitendorf] 17 hours ago [item?id=50550971] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50548865] | next [#50551202] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

If you're using grpc, http2, etc you're so much better off using Envoy proxy
You basically get to piggyback off of a large chunk of big tech's infrastructure and security work. Obviously everything has the risk of security flaws (and I kind of hate that default Envoy builds ship with half-baked filter impls) but it's a generally more mature ecosystem than projects with a lot of smaller/legacy users like nginx

I'm not 100% informed on this, but it seems very unbelievable to me that Envoy could be considered more robust or widely used than nginx at any point in history including today. I started using ConnectRPC instead of GRPC almost entirely because it didn't leave me subject to (what I consider to be) an unnecessary dependency in Envoy.

 [vote?id=50552092&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552092]
weitendorf [user?id=weitendorf] 16 hours ago [item?id=50552092] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50551940] | next [#50554752] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I guess I would just say, wordpress is also more widely used than many other projects, but that doesn't necessarily make it more robust or secure than the projects Google, Cloudflare, and security companies develop.
The main reason I see to not use Envoy is that it's very annoying to set up and build, and requires familiarity with things like grpc/proto/low level networking to get right. But if you're using grpc and http2 I think you're generally past that "infra overhead" threshold already and it's going to better serve your needs.
I have pretty much only worked at big tech or my own infrastructure startup, and did some systems/infra work at Google using Envoy for a while. I can't say it's 100% better than nginx but I can say that I've evaluated nginx and a lot of software like it (ie with a similar customer base, ecosystem, and history; like coturn) over the years for my personal use, and every time I've immediately been annoyed by constant security issues or poor http2/L4 networking support that I don't see when I use what Netflix/Google/etc use.

 [vote?id=50552608&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552608]
weitendorf [user?id=weitendorf] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552608] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50552267] | next [#50552862] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

You can implement custom http filters for envoy or use this for general web serving: https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/configuration/h... [https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/configuration/http/http_conn_man/local_reply]
The documentation, DX/UX, and discoverability for these features are pretty bad. But they work well if you know how to do it.
Implementing a custom HTTP filter itself is actually easy, I can share examples I did in the last year for a voice inference stack if anybody is interested. The problem is just that the devloop is very painful, because Envoy doesn't make it easy to know why something isn't working (this is actually most of what I did with it at Google, tons of testing/verification and compatability infra for relatively simple http stuff) and the builds take a very long time.

reply [reply?id=50552608&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350552608]

Envoy is only attractive at a higher scale.

Lucasoato [user?id=Lucasoato] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552738] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50552092] | prev [#50552267] | next [#50554752] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Does Envoy support things like rate limiting?

reply [reply?id=50552738&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350552738]

 [vote?id=50552840&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552840]
weitendorf [user?id=weitendorf] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552840] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50552738] | next [#50554752] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Yes [0]. For a single node you can just use this, it works like the Golang/language rate limiters you might already be familiar with [1]
In a multiple node/distributed architecture you need away to coordinate rate limits across nodes and not try to fight the CAP theorem.
There is a way to do this [2] [3] and although it sounds complicated the shape of the solution (delegating to a third party service that aggregates and provides client-level rate limit metrics across multiple proxies) would still be the same any way you cut it.
[0] https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/configuration/l... [https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/configuration/listeners/network_filters/local_rate_limit_filter]
[1] https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/api-v3/extensio... [https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/api-v3/extensions/filters/network/local_ratelimit/v3/local_rate_limit.proto]
[2] https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/intro/arch_over... [https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/intro/arch_overview/other_features/global_rate_limiting]
[3] https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/api-v3/config/r... [https://www.envoyproxy.io/docs/envoy/v1.15.2/api-v3/config/ratelimit/v3/rls.proto#envoy-v3-api-msg-config-ratelimit-v3-ratelimitserviceconfig]

Well that is of course because it is the _only_ proxy that seems to speak gRPC fluently. Anyone who's ever tried to configure bare nginx to serve gRPC knows this. And yet, I would wager that many stacks use both.
This requirement turns me off of gRPC entirely. Maybe Envoy truly is more secure than nginx, but it's still a whole-ass additional attack surface. It seemed to me that eliminating it if possible was best.

 [vote?id=50553980&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50553980]
weitendorf [user?id=weitendorf] 13 hours ago [item?id=50553980] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50553804] | next [#50551202] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Personally I serve all static, public content off a CDN and only serve dynamic content through Envoy after it passes AuthN/AuthZ.
I use JWT-based sessions + a remote JWKS (which is remote static content that Envoy can cache) + either a third-party IDP or dedicated login service (this being the only application logic besides Envoy's routing exposed to the public Internet). That way, every envoy auth check is immediate and stateless.
In this setup you don't really need a WAF because all unauthenticated requests redirect to signin or 403, which get strict rate limits on login. An abusive peer that sends a ton of traffic anyway gets blocked at the network/firewall level outside of Envoy. The tradeoff is you need to decide that you aren't going to do unauthenticated SSR, and I will admit this pretty complicated to set up in its entirety.

reply [reply?id=50553980&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350553980]

black3r [user?id=black3r] 19 hours ago [item?id=50549685] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50549561] | next [#50550680] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Neither Ubuntu nor Debian have released patches yet, so if you're using one of these mainstream distributions and install nginx through the distro's package manager, you can't easily "upgrade to the latest nginx" yet. You can however disable HTTP/2 for now.

 [vote?id=50550693&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50550693]
xorcist [user?id=xorcist] 18 hours ago [item?id=50550693] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50549685] | next [#50552119] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Both the Debian and the Red Hat packaging systems are awfully convenient ways to build software from source. It has to be, that's package maintainers do daily. They will install all dependencies, apply the distribution specific patches, build, and package in one operation.
Should your distributor fall of the face of the Earth and you still need to patch software, it is often the fastest way to take the source package, put the security patch in the patch directory, and run.
There is more it, such as versioning it between the last release but below the official upgrade so it's convenient when the offical one comes along, and preparing keys if you require signatures, but that's basically it.

 [vote?id=50557468&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50557468]
dvershinin [user?id=dvershinin] 4 hours ago [item?id=50557468] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50552119] | next [#50557212] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Not quite. The kernel bug is only the worker-to-root step. Code execution as the nginx worker through the two HTTP/2 CVEs needs nothing but HTTP/2 enabled. Three of the five CVEs don't need HTTP/2 at all: one is HTTP/3 with an upstream, one is resolver + variable proxy_pass + subrequests, and one is the mail proxy with auth_http.

nginx when hosed gives full access to the system.

You are aware of the massive vulnerabilities coming out across software eco-systems recently right?
Ubuntu and other distros have not kept up.
Podman container auto-updates to the latest upstream.
It is a minimal surface area given alpine images.
Security first containment.
In this day and age with AI attackers and zero-days it is absolutely daft to still treat servers as the old-school pets model.

If someone exploits nginx on host, they have a remote shell and access to everything.

> this could've been abused by anyone smart enough to monitor repos like this for exploitable bugfixes for a week
And here lies the risk of AI agents in cybersecurity: the speed of processing for monitoring, scanning, identifying security fixes in open source, writing exploits for affected versions, and attempting infiltration, is a matter of minutes now.
It is not "by anyone smart enough". It is by "anyone ill intentioned enough". They don't need to be super smart anymore.

 [vote?id=50558808&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50558808]
streetfighter64 [user?id=streetfighter64] 1 hour ago [item?id=50558808] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50557042] | next [#50549906] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

People using LLMs to hack stuff isn't really an alignment issue.
Many people, even high ups at AI companies, conflate alignment with "guardrails" (AI companies conflate intentionally as a matter of business strategy).
Alignment is just making sure the AI doesn't become a paperclip maximizer or go off and hack stuff on its own whims. This is akin to making sure a car doesn't accelerate without the accelerator being pressed.
Guardrails are about making sure people can't use the AI for nefarious purposes. This is like some sort of automatic braking to prevent people from intentionally hitting stuff with their car.
The interesting thing with AI development, is that guardrails are in some ways easier to implement than actual alignment, which is why I think many like to conflate them.

reply [reply?id=50558808&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350558808]

 [vote?id=50552137&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552137]
mort96 [user?id=mort96] 16 hours ago [item?id=50552137] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50551881] | next [#50550593] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

How about a strict anti LLM use policy for contributors? It adds what's basically a "proof of human work" challenge to opening new PRs, and effort required to review human PRs usually roughly scales in proportion to the amount of effort required to make human PRs. And human contributors who take time to make a PR manually are probably more likely to become longer term community members and not just drive-by contributors
It's not a perfect solution of course, it just takes OSS back to the fairly broken state it was pre-AI. Still, it might be an improvement and should be at least considered.

reply [reply?id=50552137&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350552137]

Tank desant is practiced because there isn’t room in the tank to fit extra people. It’s very much not safer outside of a tank than inside! It’s actually the worst of both worlds, because you don’t have the armor protection but are highly visible. The only advantage it gives over infantry assault on foot is mobility. Which is why it’s generally only practiced when there aren’t sufficient APCs/IFVs available

Not exactly true. BMPs are death traps, sure, and sitting on top of those instead of in them is rational given the risk of burning to death inside of one or being obliterated by a mine splitting the hull in half.
But the only reason to sit outside a tank rather than in one is because riding on a vehicle is safer than walking through an open field. Tank crews are not sitting outside of their tanks, and they don't have seats inside for passengers.

hunterpayne [user?id=hunterpayne] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553653] | root [#50548510] | parent [#50551225] | prev [#50551827] | next [#50549025] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Fun fact, these specific types of problems aren't possible on a JVM. Yet somehow Java isn't the right language to write a webserver in. These problems are possible in Rust and C/C++. At least its "hard" to have this type of problem in Rust.
Security is hard and is mostly about the number of engineers and amount of time spent looking at a specific implementation. When writing code, do you think about the problems of leaking information due to different operations taking specific amounts of time? That's an actual concern when writing a prod service that uses the network. And it has to be balanced against scalability and issues caused by DOSes. It isn't trivial and it isn't binary...there are always trade-offs. Nothing will ever be perfectly secure and usable and scalable.

While that is true, you should also account for exploitability. These exploits are demonstrated in lab environments which are simple and controlled. Random legit traffic will interfere with the exploit logic, so these are very hard to get in practice. Not impossible, but it's also not immediately broken.

According to https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/ve-cve-2026-123007-critical... [https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/ve-cve-2026-123007-critical-nginx-http-2-integer-underflow-leads-to-rce-fixed/] it requires 128GB to be transferred in a single connection. "a network slower than approximately 300 Mbit/s will take over the one hour connection limit threshold and will not be able to succeed"

Thankfully most of these bugs coming out seem very difficult to exploit (likely why they took so long to find). They usually become dangerous when you can build an entire chain of bugs to exploit.
But doing that requires collecting the whole chain. In the current environment we might be finding and patching these smaller bugs before they can be put in to a chain. As long as you are keeping on top of updates..

djeastm [user?id=djeastm] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552807] | root [#50551857] | parent [#50552097] | next [#50557300] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I took a quick look at The Anarchist's Tool Chest and noticed in Chapter 20 a short vignette about an expert woodworker named Troy that seemed... oddly familiar.
According to the author, Troy could do the work of three people. That would make him a 3x woodworker, I guess. And he was facing automation in the woodworking industry.
"But Troy decided to compete with the machines on their own turf. He tried to become a one-man factory. He succeeded in that. But the most efficient one-man factory cannot compete with a factory driven by a hundred men.
So no matter what Troy did, the prices for work (good or otherwise) declined. Ikea’s well-designed kitchens and Value City’s cheap junk made it impossible to survive and live well.
So Troy struggled with his business. And that’s nuts. He should have had more business than he could handle. But like many professional cabinetmakers I know, it became harder and harder for him to make ends meet every year. His cabinetmaking business is closed, and the last time
we talked, Troy was working for his wife’s successful tax business.
Who is to blame for this? We are.
We have become a culture that is obsessed with price more than any
other attribute of the things we buy. It doesn’t matter if the item is ugly,
poorly made or constructed of materials that cannot be recycled. All that
matters is if the price is low enough.
Because the price of our household objects has hit rock bottom, if an
item breaks or starts to look dated, we can throw it away and buy some-
thing else. For the first time in human history, manufactured furniture is
shockingly inexpensive.
So it’s no wonder that artisans are exiting the craft. It’s difficult to
compete against furniture that costs less than what you pay for your raw
materials.
Lesson: People cannot imitate machines and defeat them."

reply [reply?id=50552807&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350552807]

 [vote?id=50553046&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50553046]
boc [user?id=boc] 15 hours ago [item?id=50553046] | root [#50551857] | parent [#50552807] | next [#50553914] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The lesson is that most people aren't discerning consumers in multiple fields.
Appreciating good design, quality craftsmanship, having taste in food.. having these "elevated" tastes in multiple areas is very rare for humans. Take a look at a musician - they can tell you precisely what makes a quality violin, but they might not care that they eat microwave frozen dinners. You compound that across the population and the artisans will always lose out to the mass-produced slop because it's impossible to naturally get a critical mass of people to care about that quality curve. You have to sell them on it.
Troy struggled because he failed at marketing, not production.

reply [reply?id=50553046&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350553046]

 [vote?id=50554003&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50554003]
throwforfeds [user?id=throwforfeds] 13 hours ago [item?id=50554003] | root [#50551857] | parent [#50553046] | next [#50554830] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Or even single fields. I've purchased my parents the "nicer" version of things and they inevitably end up back on some crappier version. Japanese knives? Nope, still use the ones that barely cut. Ubiquiti wifi system that covered their whole house? Nope, dad unplugged something, got frustrated, and the cable company came over and put them back on the old crappy system. Nice speakers? Nope, they listen to music on their crappy TV speakers.
I think I've learned that most people don't want to put in any effort to learn or appreciate things that may be of nicer quality. Those Japanese knives didn't do any good for them because they didn't spend time learning how to use them properly. In fact one of them used them improperly and ended up needing stitches. So in the end the crappier version is no different than the nicer version, because crappy and nice are about the potential of the item, and most people don't see potential. Does this Ikea dresser hold clothes? Great, who cares that the other one is of nicer wood, the joinery is done by hand, and it'll last multiple generations.

jkingsman [user?id=jkingsman] 16 hours ago [item?id=50552086] | root [#50551857] | parent [#50551955] | next [#50552440] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Becoming a carpenter, and sentiments of "I'm going to live in the forest" or "I'm going to be a farmer" are usually expressions of fatigue with technology, and a desire to be free of their problems; thus, an expressed urge to pursue a career where technology is not a major component. It can also sometimes carry a desire to have one's labor output be tangible and physical (i.e. a worked wood object) rather than abstract, as so much programming/operations/etc. work often is.
The comment you replied to is shortcutting the joke, assuming the common riff of becoming a carpenter is well known, and jumping ahead to asking about tutorials for woodworking as part of that process.

pixl97 [user?id=pixl97] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548776] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548492] | next [#50552337] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Both are true. The problem is security is a much harder job than attacking.
Worse patching adds instability into a system, especially when you don't have much time to test.
Also in the past attackers were willing to sit on exploits longer and not do things with them, now they have to act fast before someone else finds it, leading to more events faster wearing your security and patching teams out, and making users life suck.
Things are going to get much worse before they get better.

 [vote?id=50556991&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50556991]
ActorNightly [user?id=ActorNightly] 6 hours ago [item?id=50556991] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548776] | prev [#50553749] | next [#50550516] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> The problem is security is a much harder job than attacking.
Nope.
Let say Im running a vulnerable Ngnix server. All that would take to stop any and all attacks is a simple firewall that sees anything that even remotely looks like shell code (i.e non ascii characters, ROP patterns, strings of noops, e.t.c) close that connection,blacklist that ip.
>Also in the past attackers were willing to sit on exploits longer and not do things with them,
Also nope. The reason why you didn't see many exploits is because they are mostly useless unless you have resources. You can take a gander through all the CVEs that AIs are finding. 99% of them are either DDOS with some possibilty of remote code execution only under very particular conditions, or require already user level code execution to get to root.
There are a few sprinklings of truly bad ones, but even for those, a good portion of them that exist on critical systems are protected by secondary things, like firewalls that detect shell code and close the connection and ban the IP for some time. True network security will never be not needed, as humans are still the most vulnerable vector, and if you don't have systems in play that can counter bad actors, then you are asking to get hacked.
All of this AI finding exploits is basically fear mongering that is going to be used to sell AI products that secure systems.

reply [reply?id=50556991&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350556991]

 [vote?id=50550516&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50550516]
devy [user?id=devy] 18 hours ago [item?id=50550516] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548776] | prev [#50556991] | next [#50552337] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Except, human in the loop in control - blue team is not defending red team fast enough that most the CVSS v4.0 Critical CVEs aren't easily patched in coordinated fashion. Many times, almost impractical or impossible to patch at all, with decades of intertwined dependency tree and their ecosystem.
Collectively, we owed about 40+ years of tech debt on non-secure code (e.g. automotive industry, industrial environments) that will be torched in the next few years. Luckily, AI will generate code faster enough to make that plausible.

 [vote?id=50552337&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552337]
djha-skin [user?id=djha-skin] 16 hours ago [item?id=50552337] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548492] | prev [#50548776] | next [#50556802] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

AI provides the capabilities to secure the internet, maybe, but the internet is secured when site owners choose to secure it.
That's not happening at nearly the same pace, both for assymetry between what it takes to break something and what it takes to fix it, but also because that fix must navigate bureaucracies. Also, the leaders need to actually care, both of which are hurdles. Bureaucratic navigation and consensus finding has not sped up at all with AI.
Compare that to independent script kiddies with $600/mo Claude subscriptions able to find 0-days in an hour and write a worm to exploit it in four.

 [vote?id=50549324&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50549324]
thaumaturgy [user?id=thaumaturgy] 19 hours ago [item?id=50549324] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548492] | prev [#50550449] | next [#50549669] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Man, is it aggravating to read fantasies like this, as someone who's trying to juggle a full-time job and hosted public services for other people at the same time.
There are two internets. One of them is open, less-commercial, and largely built and maintained by people whose first job is not the work they do to maintain this internet. That group includes a ton of open source software, millions of small servers scattered around the world (virtually private or otherwise), and is generally best-effort.
The other internet is fully commercialized, often ad-driven, fully integral to the data broker economy, and centralized behind a few companies that pay as few people as possible to satisfy the occasional PR statement that, "we take security seriously and...".
The next stable equilibrium here is not "we get a secure and open internet". The next stable equilibrium here is, "we have no open internet, and the internet is now managed by just a few companies."
We're already halfway there. Google and Microsoft combined already account for somewhere around 30% to 40% of email traffic. 30% of all websites are now behind Cloudflare. A few companies dominate social media traffic. Amazon and Shopify combined are half of US e-commerce.
Every single short-response-window exploit like this just pushes a few more percentage points of traffic into a handful of companies. Cheer for that, I guess.
When I have time to daydream, it's increasingly about building a new, underground internet, and how best to disadvantage bots and BigCo conglomeration from the start.

reply [reply?id=50549324&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350549324]

I wonder whether part of the solution is not using the same software as big tech. For them rapid development is worth extra maintenance. Things like the efficiencies of http2 over 1.1 actually matter. Maybe the rest of us should be using BSDs or different Linux distros (Alpine? Void? Slackware?), different web servers, turn off features we do not need (e.g. not disable http2, or at least not allow both 1.1 and 2 as that is clearly extra attack surface).

No, AI is just exposing 30+ years of sloppy code written in unsafe languages.
People joke about the "Rewrite it in Rust" crowd, but that call is rooted in an unease anyone with a sceptic mind has had about all the C and C++ code we've got running our infrastructure running around tracing pointers with little lifetime- or bounds-checking. (Yes, it's a runtime cost but it's 2 CVE's, one for each class)

For eight years, MongoDB shipped a default configuration that left it opened to the internet with no password.
Obviously, AI makes these things easier to find and exploit, but this isn't even close to the first time that 'thousands' of hobbyist (or small business or just not very competent) server admins are going to get rootkitted en masse. I worked at medium-sized infra-as-a-service company when the above was discovered and there were definitely thousands of compromised machines just there.

 [vote?id=50548629&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50548629]
vlovich123 [user?id=vlovich123] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548629] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548569] | next [#50549993] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I don’t know any systems programming language that doesn’t ignore integer overflow/underflow in release builds. IMHO this is a CPU design flaw - integer operations should carry a flag indicating if the operation is allowed to wrap or if it should generate a trap. Then there wouldn’t be a performance penalty for this and languages like Rust could have it be trapped by default.

reply [reply?id=50548629&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350548629]

 [vote?id=50548674&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50548674]
tptacek [user?id=tptacek] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548674] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548629] | next [#50548920] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

No. Memory safe languages still have integer handling issues, but the consequences are different. In a memory-unsafe language, integer mishandling often nets out to bad copies and memory corruption.

I wouldn’t be sure that a Rust equivalent would be immune because it’s not hard to imagine an underflow/overflow from reaching an unsafe block that’s used for performance (the implicated TLS path would be a hotspot worthy of such a choice).

 [vote?id=50548818&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50548818]
tptacek [user?id=tptacek] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548818] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548780] | next [#50550781] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This is the thing about `unsafe` blocks: they're not memory safe. It's right there in the name.
I'm not making a Rust advocacy argument. I'm just saying: it's true that integer handling issues are endemic to mainstream programming languages, but the integer handling itself is almost never the actual sharp end of the vulnerability.

reply [reply?id=50548818&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350548818]

 [vote?id=50556247&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50556247]
vlovich123 [user?id=vlovich123] 8 hours ago [item?id=50556247] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548818] | next [#50550781] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Android had an integer overflow vulnerability where it could be tricked into verifying the signing of one file but install another for execution. Boeing 787 Dreamliner had a bug where after 248 it's 32-bit counter overflowed triggering a "fail-safe" mode that shut down all electrical power to the aircraft.
You're right it's more rare but the results can still be catastrophic.

AnthonyMouse [user?id=AnthonyMouse] 18 hours ago [item?id=50550781] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548674] | prev [#50548780] | next [#50548920] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Isn't "memory corruption" typically the fast path to RCE? The attacker gets to overwrite a function pointer, or a regular pointer that then lets them overwrite something else, or the length field that will then cause a buffer overrun even in a "safe" language because the run-time length is now different than the one in the compile-time check, etc.

reply [reply?id=50550781&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350550781]

 [vote?id=50552114&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552114]
tptacek [user?id=tptacek] 16 hours ago [item?id=50552114] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50550781] | next [#50548920] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Memory corruption -> RCE.
Integer handling mistake -> memory corruption -> RCE.
That's the pattern. In a memory-safe language, the integer handling error has no link to memory corruption (though it will to other logic bugs!).

reply [reply?id=50552114&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350552114]

10000truths [user?id=10000truths] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548920] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548629] | prev [#50548674] | next [#50550098] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Zig traps arithmetic overflow/underflow when compiling in ReleaseSafe.

reply [reply?id=50548920&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350548920]

 [vote?id=50550098&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50550098]
benlivengood [user?id=benlivengood] 18 hours ago [item?id=50550098] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548629] | prev [#50548920] | next [#50549546] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

X86 had the INTO instruction to trigger a fault if the Overflow flag is set, so not quite a per-instruction bit but more of a suffix. JO is also fairly straightforward for compilers to add on important arithmetic operations. We can probably blame C entirely for this; only in C23 are there intrinsics for overflow detection on arithmetic.

 [vote?id=50552885&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552885]
Creamsicle47 [user?id=Creamsicle47] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552885] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50551997] | next [#50553371] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Agents are simply faster.
Harnesses allow LLMs to build & process large stores of data to reuse later. Imagine an agent that spends 3 days breaking into company A, records what it learned during that process, letting it break into company B in just 3 hours.
Defenders win by having the best security 24/7 and must trust that other services they depend on are doing the same.
An attacker wins when they can breach any defender's security.

reply [reply?id=50552885&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350552885]

ActorNightly [user?id=ActorNightly] 6 hours ago [item?id=50557023] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548622] | prev [#50552946] | next [#50549200] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Nothing to do with language, everything to do with lack of standards. You can write perfectly unsafe Rust code.
Dunno if you have ever worked in software engineering, but if you have, count the times you have seen a backend server api written without any input sanitization or checking. Most of these exploits can be defeated by checks on inputs. I.e, no "Alex\x90\x90\x90\x90\xcc\xcc\xcc\xcc", is not a valid input for someones name.

acdha [user?id=acdha] 20 hours ago [item?id=50549200] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50548622] | prev [#50557023] | next [#50548506] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The bigger problem is lack of liability: we knew C sucked in the 90s, too, but we had three decades of over-confident dudes saying that was user error and _they_ were smart enough to use C correctly.
Then Rust came along and those dudes either tried to say it was too hard (odd contrast, that) or it’d be too much work to rewrite things.
I’m hoping that LLMs will lower the cost of porting for enough people but what would really help is shedding the idea that the status quo is sustainable. Having consequences for sticking with C would help but we need a way to get companies to pay to migrate without destroying open source.

 [vote?id=50551069&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50551069]
antonvs [user?id=antonvs] 17 hours ago [item?id=50551069] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50549270] | next [#50554286] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Aside from the overhead of borrow checking, what about the rest of the language sucks, in your view?
To me, its type system and traits design make it one of the nicest mainstream languages. I'd rather be using a functional language, like Haskell or an ML, and I do sometimes, but I don't pick my work based on that. I work regularly with Java, Go, Typescript, and Python, and out of those I find Rust significantly superior on several dimensions.
Python is terrible, Go is basically weakly-typed Python, Typescript barely deserves comment other than that it's impressive that such a fancy type system was bolted onto Javascript, and Java is basically COBOL at this point - reliable, but clunky and showing its age. Attempts like Kotlin to leverage the JVM aren't very convincing.
Gleam looks interesting but I haven't had a chance to work with it yet, and it's hardly mainstream.
You mentioned Ada/SPARK, but SPARK is not really for general purpose programming, and Ada in general has a similar issue to Java, but worse because of its age. It's as if someone really liked PL/I but decided it needed more features and a syntax closer to COBOL.

 [vote?id=50556733&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50556733]
antonvs [user?id=antonvs] 6 hours ago [item?id=50556733] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50551872] | prev [#50555041] | next [#50554302] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I was talking about coding effort - reasoning about borrowing, lifetime annotations, and the fact that this can make refactoring more involved because lifting arbitrary blocks of code, especially when closures are involved, can require extra work.
Someone who's saying "the rest of the language sucks" may be thinking of something like that, hence my question above.
But when it comes to performance overhead, programming around the constraints of the borrow checker can introduce runtime costs that wouldn't otherwise be necessary - e.g. when cloning is used to handle borrowing conflicts.
Even if, in theory, it's possible to restructure a program to avoid performance impacts, in practice people don't always do that, not least because more efficient solutions may not always be obvious. This comes back to my point about coding effort.
I spent a bunch of time, pre-LLM, to try to replace a bunch of unsafe blocks scattered throughout an ML inference codebase, that someone had used to achieve maximum performance. I wasn't able to get a version that performed as well.
This kind of thing may not matter if you're writing e.g. ordinary web services, but for more performance-critical code, it can be an issue. Of course, `unsafe` is always an option to deal with that, but that undermines Rust's guarantees.

reply [reply?id=50556733&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350556733]

 [vote?id=50554286&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50554286]
dboreham [user?id=dboreham] 13 hours ago [item?id=50554286] | root [#50548180] | parent [#50549200] | prev [#50549270] | next [#50548506] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This really isn't true. It's true that those people saying those things existed, but much more recently. Back when all the C/C++ legacy code we depend on today was being written it was the performance penalty imposed by safety that forced developers down that path, not some lack of interest in other options. We were also transitioning from a world where a fair bit of systems code was written in even lower level languages than C (assembler, BLISS, BCPL). The world we're in today effectively began around 1975 when people figured out how to make a C compiler run on an 11/45. All the decisions were pretty much locked in from there on.

Bender [user?id=Bender] 13 hours ago [item?id=50553999] | root [#50553406] | parent [#50553851] | next [#50554135] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Sadly it is not but one can disable it easily enough.

 echo 'install sctp /bin/false' > /etc/modprobe.d/no-sctp.conf
 echo 'install dccp /bin/false' > /etc/modprobe.d/no-dccp.conf
 echo 'install rds /bin/false' > /etc/modprobe.d/no-rds.conf

chlorion [user?id=chlorion] 13 hours ago [item?id=50554020] | root [#50552069] | parent [#50552385] | prev [#50553628] | next [#50555136] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Having RCEs in general is fine. It's the rate and severity that matter.
We have evidence that large unsafe codebases suffer from more and more severe CVEs than those who are not in memory unsafe languages.
In other fields of engineering, people use stats and actually measure things like this, and use the results to determine what to do. They don't just assume something is better because its "battle tested" (whatever that even means?).

I have the same biases in me and more. This one is confirmation bias, I think. Using new information to reinforce existing belief.
I switched to Linux more than 10 years ago, and when something goes wrong, I'm still like "fucking linux". I think it's great to point out these biases, like you did. Us humans are both surprisingly sharp, and woefully inaccurate when we point out observances in the world.

With regards to software, my personal stance is that the deeper I go in layers, the more boring ol' reliable I prefer.

jeroenhd [user?id=jeroenhd] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553648] | root [#50552069] | parent [#50553120] | next [#50552914] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

CVE-2026-98432 also applies to non-MPTCP connections. That's why disabling MPTCP was actively discouraged as a workaround (low level firewalls that clear all options do work, though, and they'd also work fine with Caddy).
Plus, Caddy itself wasn't vulnerable in this specific case. There wasn't much the Caddy folks could've done to protect against a problem in the Linux kernel itself.

Integer underflow again. Programming languages should trap on integer overflows and not ignore them. Don't care about legacy CPUs not having a trap for this - they will go sooner or later.

 [vote?id=50549341&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50549341]
deathanatos [user?id=deathanatos] 19 hours ago [item?id=50549341] | parent [#50548554] | next [#50547678] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Rust has a compile time option, though to my immense disappointment, it defaults to on only in debug mode. (You can opt into it for release, if desired.)
Python simply can't overflow, since its integral type is not a fixed width.
But it is the sort of correctness thing that I do wish languages would pay more attention to.

codedokode [user?id=codedokode] 10 hours ago [item?id=50555499] | root [#50548554] | parent [#50550030] | next [#50550159] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Overflows are dangerous by itself. Assume you keep the order total price as uint32. Someone orders a spaceship for 2.2B credits, and then adds another one which is not an expected scenario. The total counter overflows and now you have sold 2 expensive spaceships at 99% discount.

 [vote?id=50558404&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50558404]
Cthulhu_ [user?id=Cthulhu_] 2 hours ago [item?id=50558404] | root [#50547678] | parent [#50547875] | next [#50548182] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Personally I think it's a good thing - many vulnerabilities are being found and fixed now, people are wide awake and paying attention to them being found so updating is quicker and easier, and since code is finite, so are vulnerabilities so in, say, a year or so there's going to be the status quo of LLM vulnerability scanners run by dozens of companies running 24/7 on every software ever... unable to find anything.
So yeah it sucks right now, there's outages, probable PII theft left right and center (but that's nothing new at this point), millions / billions in damages and lost productivity from Office365 and co being offline for a day or two... but it's still manageable, the world isn't on fire, and it's better to get all that out of the way now than in an actual crisis.

The observation was "if one person found the bug with LLMs, other people are also going to find the bug". Whether it was due to communication between instances of the agent or just due to the predictability of an individual agent is not clear and there is not much evidence for the former.

Consider a world in which the only people that get to know about it are the people who discovered it in the first place.
Consider a world in which corporations like the Ubuntu Foundation or Microsoft experience zero public pressure to fix such vulnerabilities but instead fix it only when a major customer notices their servers getting hit.
Imagine if instead of "you've got 30/60/90 days to fix this thing I noticed before I post it publically" it was "okay, well, take your time I guess, I just hope North Korea doesn't notice the same issue I noticed, which is especially likely because they have far more resources than I or my small team does to point at discovering this kind of thing, while you're dragging your feet fixing it."
The reason people want shorter disclosure windows is because the shorter the window is the faster things get fixed for the average users and smaller companies.

reply [reply?id=50550451&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350550451]

8by3 [user?id=8by3] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548439] | prev [#50547891] | next [#50557995] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

FreeBSD is unpatched,
I got worried for a sec, then remembered all my nginx lives behind HaProxy and it looks like nginx is only vulnerable if its going http2/TLS directly.
Now I sit and wait for an HaProxy announcement...
ps. if you are on FreeBSD and doing TLS directly
sed -i.bak -E 's/^([[:space:]]server[[:space:]]\{)$/\1\
 http2 off;/' /usr/local/etc/nginx/nginx.conf
;service nginx restart

tripleee [user?id=tripleee] 16 hours ago [item?id=50552085] | parent [#50551800] | next [#50551883] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It's possible that AI is going to be better at finding and exploiting systems than at securing them. It's not really an even playing field. On the one hand you have to somehow ensure that you've patched everything when there's no definite list of everything, the other side just needs to find one overlooked way in.
At the rate of development, also, will AI developed in 6 months from now be able to break a system that was secured by todays AI?

 [vote?id=50552326&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50552326]
jtmarmon [user?id=jtmarmon] 16 hours ago [item?id=50552326] | root [#50551800] | parent [#50552085] | next [#50555954] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It's not possible. It generally requires more work to find an exploit than to patch the (known) exploit.
Now, you could argue that writing secure code ex-ante is harder than discovering an exploit. But you can hypothetically run the same "exploit finding" process on a first pass of code before it ships (e.g on the PR) given low enough token costs, which is where we're headed.

The better LLMs get at finding vulnerabilities, the more obvious it will be to bolt them into software development processes to find the bugs prior to release.
With enterprise frontier LLM models, there is no longer an “other side.” Everyone is just using what they can rent from a few big suppliers. You can too.

 [vote?id=50555821&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50555821]
userbinator [user?id=userbinator] 9 hours ago [item?id=50555821] | prev [#50551800] | next [#50558628] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

HTTP/2 again, not surprisingly. The curse of complexity strikes again. When I first saw the spec, I knew it would be far more difficult to implement correctly than 1.1 or 1.0. While 1.x is not ideal either (parsing a protocol that involves free-form text with no fixed limits is always a bit scary), at least it has a much smaller attack surface.
On the bright side, perhaps "security review" of code which involves throwing it at an AI and fixing what it finds before releasing will become a normal practice.
"There are two ways of constructing a software design: One way is to make it so simple that there are obviously no deficiencies, and the other way is to make it so complicated that there are no obvious deficiencies." - C.A.R. Hoare

imhoguy [user?id=imhoguy] 17 hours ago [item?id=50551633] | root [#50547781] | parent [#50548462] | prev [#50549057] | next [#50548854] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Who is sane enough in 2026 to expose distro default services to the Internet?
I wouldn't expect any distro maintainers to react in hours. Distros are too complex to dump updates in rush.
Start to use Docker/Podman/systemd-nspawn.. and isolate public services to necessary needs (static binaries, drop capabilities, default drop rule firewall between containers, r/o storage, access logs bind mounted as rw file, memory temp dir with noexec). Container images are patched the quickest.

layer8 [user?id=layer8] 19 hours ago [item?id=50549732] | root [#50547781] | parent [#50548280] | next [#50552671] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The point is that it must have been deliberately enabled. TLS will generally be enabled out of necessity, whereas the chances for HTTP/2 are much lower, and your NGINX configuration will explicitly indicate if it’s enabled. If you don’t see “http2” in your configuration, then you know you are fine.

 [vote?id=50551009&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50551009]
tkz1312 [user?id=tkz1312] 17 hours ago [item?id=50551009] | parent [#50550629] | next [#50550748] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I now run all my network facing C code via fil-c. I have limited needs (ssh, static file server), but for those it works very well. I personally am more in favour of making existing battle tested C safer via fil-c or formal verification, than I am of rewriting the world in rust.
New code means new bugs, and losing the many years of accumulated bug fixes and hardening present in many critical pieces of our infra by rewriting in rust is not a desirable tradeoff. The new world we live in where fv has become many orders of magnitude cheaper also makes rust seem much less attractive.

reply [reply?id=50551009&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350551009]

 [vote?id=50551908&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50551908]
safercplusplus [user?id=safercplusplus] 16 hours ago [item?id=50551908] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50551027] | next [#50553482] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

In theory, scpptool provides another solution with different performance, compatibility and portability trade-offs. Unlike Fil-C, it is not "fanatically compatible" with existing C code. But in theory, with maybe some minor adjustments to the code (and maybe less-minor adjustments to the build system), you can auto-translate your existing C code base to an essentially memory-safe subset of C++ at build-time [1].
While generally more of a hassle than Fil-C, the scpptool approach provides an incremental path to optimal performance (for performance-sensitive sections of the code) via migration to a (statically-enforced) memory-safe subset of C++. And unlike Fil-C, the scpptool solution essentially maintains the portability of the original code, as well as its deterministic performance and resource usage (i.e. no GC). It also supports ABI compatibility via "unsafe"/"check-suppression" annotations. The scpptool approach is not so much an alternative to Fil-C, as it is an optional path forward for C (or C++) code bases that may (or may not) be already using Fil-C for memory safety.
[1] https://github.com/duneroadrunner/SaferCPlusPlus-AutoTransla... [https://github.com/duneroadrunner/SaferCPlusPlus-AutoTranslation2/blob/master/README.md]

 [vote?id=50551563&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50551563]
dralley [user?id=dralley] 17 hours ago [item?id=50551563] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50551297] | next [#50551507] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Nobody is saying that it needs to be rushed. Cloudflare started moving towards Rust more than a decade ago and (shortly after heartbleed happened) and progressively rolled bits out over the course of years. I'm sure there were problems here and there but it's hardly been a drag on them.
And parts of that involved specifically replacing Nginx in their architecture, so it's hardly irrelevant here.
https://blog.cloudflare.com/20-percent-internet-upgrade/ [https://blog.cloudflare.com/20-percent-internet-upgrade/]
https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-built-pingora-the-proxy-t... [https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-built-pingora-the-proxy-that-connects-cloudflare-to-the-internet]
> With those assurances we can focus more on how a change to our service will interact with other services or a customer's origin. We can develop features at a higher cadence and not be burdened by memory safety and hard to diagnose crashes.
> When crashes do occur an engineer needs to spend time to diagnose how it happened and what caused it. Since Pingora's inception we’ve served a few hundred trillion requests and have yet to crash due to our service code.
> In fact, Pingora crashes are so rare we usually find unrelated issues when we do encounter one. Recently we discovered a kernel bug soon after our service started crashing. We've also discovered hardware issues on a few machines, in the past ruling out rare memory bugs caused by our software even after significant debugging was nearly impossible.

If we're suggesting alternative web servers, I have had a pleasant experience with caddyserver (Go). It's been around 10+ years, has essentially transparent TLS certificate provisioning, and its config syntax is easy to understand. Even using AI to help vet configs, it's readable. It's packaged everywhere and has lots of plugins for things, though I only need the base package for my frontend proxy usecase.

As someone that was a developer for a long time what is the problem with C? How does going to rust mitigate this? Why is having the stuff in C a security challenge?

reply [reply?id=50551082&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350551082]

 [vote?id=50551921&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50551921]
Delk [user?id=Delk] 16 hours ago [item?id=50551921] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50551082] | next [#50551116] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It's very easy to accidentally write buffer overflows, use-after-frees or other memory-safety bugs in C. Modern tooling can probably catch some of these better than in the days of yore but the fundamentals of the language are still the same.
If e.g. the standard array type in a language has built-in bounds checks that error out when you try to write to an index that's outside of the array, you can't trivially create buffer overflows simply by writing to the wrong index or by trying to write n + 10 bytes into an n-byte array.
But C in itself has no guardrails against something like

 char buffer[100];
 strcpy(buffer, some_user_supplied_string_with_200_chars);

Of course most memory safety bugs aren't trivial like that since C programmers have hopefully learned to avoid the simple mistakes. But there can be more complicated and difficult to spot cases, and C has no guardrails against those either.

reply [reply?id=50551921&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350551921]

If a bug is found before its released... Then that's not a 0-day?
The name refers to the number of days since public release. "Exploitable 0 days since release". If you found a bug before release, then that's... Just a bug picked it up in normal development lifecycle.

I read 0 days like "Exploitable 0 days since _discovery_". Also, it's only a bug in the normal lifecycle if the discoverer tells the developer making it. If they discover it and then have hours and days to perfect a program that exploits it, then starts running immediately when it is deployed or released, that's a new kind of "zero day". Makes you want to start differentiating what "0 days" mean, though.

 [vote?id=50556823&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50556823]
pjmlp [user?id=pjmlp] 6 hours ago [item?id=50556823] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50556540] | next [#50551346] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The solution was not to take C into widespread use in first place.
Note that even C creators, first came up with Alef for Plan 9, which did not work out due to lack of automatic resource management (on Rob Pike's own words), and then Limbo for Inferno, which by then meant C main purpose was the OS itself and DisVM/JIT implementation.
Even on classical UNIX, there used to be a tendency that many userspace tools were to be written on top of scripting like AWK, sed, tr, Perl,... not everything to be C.
In terms of systems programming safety that people wonder about Zig, was already there in languages like JOVIAL, PL/I and NEWP, among others, a decade before C came to be.
The solution is not to write new code in C, unless there is no alternative, like coding on UNIX/POSIX kernels (UNIX based on Ada or Pascal dialects like did not fair that well, e.g. Domain/OS).
Not everything has to be Rust, it can be Ada, Modula-2 (now on GCC), D, Swift, FreePascal/Delphi, Zig, Odin, C++ (proper C++ with standard library, not code C with C++ compiler), C# AOT, Java AOT, Go, .... whatever depending on the use case.

 [vote?id=50550928&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50550928]
tgma [user?id=tgma] 17 hours ago [item?id=50550928] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50550858] | next [#50553213] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It is true that mileage is a good proxy for finding bugs, but this is isomorphic to the fundamental differences between testing v. verification. You can prove absence of classes of bugs with verification. Obviously there can be other logic bugs in newer software. No question. The issue is the memory-corruption bugs in socket-facing daemons can have catastrophic consequences, especially in light of chaining kernel vulnerabilities which defeats the privilege drop that nginx does.
So I'd say it is about a more holistic risk-analysis not just handwaving "all code can have bugs, duh!"

reply [reply?id=50550928&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350550928]

A more holistic risk-analysis would also need to consider the fact that a high-performance webserver written in Rust may also have memory safety issue due to use of "unsafe" features or unsafe dependencies, may be affected by memory safety issues in the standard library, may also have catastrophic logic errors, may be more affect by supply chain risks due to the use of a language-level package manager, and may have a larger patch gap.

ux266478 [user?id=ux266478] 16 hours ago [item?id=50551875] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50551139] | next [#50551169] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Not in the slightest. It's like pointing out that a Python script calling out to binaries written in C doesn't have the memory safety of Python's GC. Which is obviously the case, and if you're a serious developer it's a failure point you're very mindful of.
On the Android point, I remember those metrics were highly flawed and didn't account for the inequality of substructural type ergonomics in different contexts. You can get very nice metrics by rewriting things that fit to the grain of safe Rust, which gives you an inaccurate picture of how much unsafe code is "real world". LLMs have changed the field here, but it's worth pointing out that blog post you're referencing was more self-congratulatory marketing than serious science.
Also problematically, I remember it being highly misleading in that the actual observed result was lower severity in vulnerabilities, but a very minimal effect on the total number over all. That's certainly significant, but it wasn't how it presented itself, which is rather problematic if you expect people to take it seriously as objective research.
Measurements probably real, but not worth citing. It's not like on their own blog they're under usual academic journal pressures to overclaim results, p-hack, and engage in other misleading behaviors.

uecker [user?id=uecker] 7 hours ago [item?id=50556450] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50551279] | next [#50556279] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Research is not just making some plots and posting them on the internet. A proper scientific studies would need to be done much more carefully, if you want to have any chance to get meaningful results, because there is a lot which can go wrong at any level. This is something learned people learned the hard way in empirical sciences. In particular, in this setting I do not see how one could ever properly control for confounding factors. Such a study should also be peer reviewed. Then repeated by others. Also if there are commercial interests mixed-up this needs to be questioned carefully. Finally, I just note the data presented seems mostly about C++ not C, so is not clear how this applies to C. Not separating this would also already be an obvious methodological problem. This is also connected to the problem that the underlying data is not made available, and not properly described in sufficient detail. Etc. (Not a full list, just to make it clear this is not even remotely science).

Which is to say, you have constructed a standard that lets you dismiss virtually any empirical finding. It seems like the goalposts will just keep moving as Rust replaces insecure languages.
With differences this large it is something completely different to teasing out a P value of 0.05 from messy data. Which you attempted to conflate the findings with.
> We adopted Rust for its security and are seeing a 1000x reduction in memory safety vulnerability density compared to Android’s C and C++ code. But the biggest surprise was Rust's impact on software delivery. With Rust changes having a 4x lower rollback rate and spending 25% less time in code review, the safer path is now also the faster one.

 [vote?id=50556594&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50556594]
uecker [user?id=uecker] 7 hours ago [item?id=50556594] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50556279] | next [#50551719] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

In my view, the Rust argument is more like that driving (in C) can not possible be safe because there is always the inherent risk of steering into an obstacle, so we have to abandon it completely and only use the local public rail system (which entirely eliminate this class of steering errors). But this is argument made based on the state of the technology in 1950s without considering that seatbelts and other modern techniques, driver tests, speed limits can make driving a lot safer (as seen today when compared to the 1950s).
Seatbelts are more comparable to activating bounds checking in C compared to switching to another language. because they are a minor inconvenience with a huge positive security effects, but do not eliminate all risk. Rust is more like switching to public rail system, because it does eliminate certain risks completely (all related to steering), but not others, and then also limits you in various way, so you may still need to use a car for the final meters (e.g. C or "unsafe" Rust).
And this comparison is consistent with the idea that the public rail system really is safer to use with regard to traffic accidents, so captures the idea that Rust actually has safety benefits. But IMHO the framing and marketing is highly misleading.

any serious engineering discipline eliminates the possibility of human error wherever possible, especially when it results in dangerous effects. and yet in software there are people who prefer exposing others to those dangerous effects because of a personal preference for a 50 year old tool.

 [vote?id=50551719&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50551719]
dralley [user?id=dralley] 16 hours ago [item?id=50551719] | root [#50550629] | parent [#50551055] | prev [#50556279] | next [#50553213] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This argument is cope.
In theory, that could be possible. In practice, there have been many many case studies demonstrating the opposite and not that many case studies demonstrating in favor of your argument.
e.g. Cloudflare replaced Nginx with Pingora: https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-built-pingora-the-proxy-t... [https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-built-pingora-the-proxy-that-connects-cloudflare-to-the-internet]
> With those assurances we can focus more on how a change to our service will interact with other services or a customer's origin. We can develop features at a higher cadence and not be burdened by memory safety and hard to diagnose crashes.
> When crashes do occur an engineer needs to spend time to diagnose how it happened and what caused it. Since Pingora's inception we’ve served a few hundred trillion requests and have yet to crash due to our service code.
> In fact, Pingora crashes are so rare we usually find unrelated issues when we do encounter one. Recently we discovered a kernel bug soon after our service started crashing. We've also discovered hardware issues on a few machines, in the past ruling out rare memory bugs caused by our software even after significant debugging was nearly impossible.
The CVE counts for new parts of the Linux kernel written in Rust are much lower than the (recent) CVE counts for even mature, years old parts of the Linux kernel written in C, to say nothing of the historical record.

On the other hand I've never been so proactive about maintaining security on my homelab! I did a full audit of my self-hosted services with claude code, strengthened the isolation of all my docker services, I'm running coding agents in sandboxed microVMs now, my firewall settings are better, I set up separate VLAN for IoT, I get security notifications on my dependencies, etc. etc.
A lot of stuff that I avoided for years I finally feel like I really need to do now but it also has never been easier to do.

tancop [user?id=tancop] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548533] | prev [#50553213] | next [#50549596] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Everybody here talking about going back to HTTP/1.1, it's a good stop gap solution but we should really think about moving to HTTP3-only as fast as possible. Changing over is easy, if your server doesn't support it just put it behind a reverse proxy that does.
Unlike IPv6 it doesn't need any OS or ISP support, just a browser that's newer than 2020. Everyone outside of air gap networks and government sites should be switching.

gkbrk [user?id=gkbrk] 19 hours ago [item?id=50549820] | root [#50548533] | parent [#50549343] | next [#50551006] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Because any HTTP spec after 1.1 is incredibly complicated. You can implement HTTP/1 in half a day, and HTTP/1.1 in a day. But HTTP/2 will probably take you a month and HTTP/3 might take something like 6 months.

reply [reply?id=50549820&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350549820]

 [vote?id=50551106&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50551106]
Liuser [user?id=Liuser] 17 hours ago [item?id=50551106] | root [#50548533] | parent [#50549820] | next [#50550729] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It boggles my mind that people are patting themselves on the back for staying on old tech stacks and using a discovered vulnerability as further justification of their decision to stay on h1.1. The response is to patch and move on if your environment requires h2; many large orgs these days use microservices which use h2 (grpc) as its network.
There are many legitimate use cases for h2 (eg bidi streaming, latency improvements) and h3 (eg seamless network switching).
If you don’t need these features and just serve vanilla text webpages that’s also fine to just use h1.1. Blanket statements that h1.1 is better is just, wrong. Context is important on choosing what’s best for your environment.

reply [reply?id=50551106&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350551106]

doodlesdev [user?id=doodlesdev] 17 hours ago [item?id=50551037] | root [#50548533] | parent [#50550386] | next [#50555591] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

That's not the point. The point is that the simpler the protocol is to implement, the simpler it is to validate and harden the implementations. A HTTP/1.1 implementation can be 10x to 30x smaller than an equivalently well-rounded HTTP/3 implementation.

well it's a good time to pitch tech debt and infrastructure cleanup. If you aren't making use of least-privilege permissions, service-mesh, netpols etc. this is the time to add all of that.

 [vote?id=50548233&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50548233]
whartung [user?id=whartung] 21 hours ago [item?id=50548233] | root [#50547958] | parent [#50547992] | prev [#50548055] | next [#50549549] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Actually, this is good I think.
One, as the RCEs are coming out, admins are on edge and paying more attention, so more likely to catch the annoucement on their platform. "Oh, that's on PHP, we don't use this. Oh, wait, another one?"
Two, all of these hitting at once, while they may expand the surface for attackers, it also spreads their attention. They only have so much time and bandwidth. When a new shiny RCE hits, they need to consider "do we continue to try an prosecute the old one or jump on this bandwagon".
So, in the sense, it can actually slow down the actual potential impact of the revelations.
We can only hope this gives users time to get their systems up to date before they come knocking.
I mean, I know its a reach, but...who knows.

 [vote?id=50548278&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50548278]
K0nserv [user?id=K0nserv] 21 hours ago [item?id=50548278] | root [#50547794] | parent [#50548186] | next [#50548707] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

This fixes the integer underflow for CVE-2026-123007, and it's true that the underflow can happen in any language, notwithstanding whether it's UB or not. The issue is what happens as a consequence of this underflow, in this case: out of bounds copy, which is specific to C.

nh2 [user?id=nh2] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548742] | root [#50547794] | parent [#50548278] | next [#50550947] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Some languages make it significantly easier than C to just forbid integer overflow/underflow entirely.
Given that on modern CPUs, cycles are quite fast, and memory latency is quite high, always-checked integer operations sound like an extremely good idea.

Sure, I usually use `overflow-checks = true` in Rust for this reason. However, it is still true that the overflow is not what caused the issue here. In Rust it would've paniced on the out of bounds copy, even with `overflow-checks = false`.

It is not specific to C. You could have bounds checking also in C, and you can have non-checked indexing also in many other languages, including Rust. I would agree though that many other language make it harder to not have the bounds checking, but the bigger question is why this is not done C (and the answer that this is very hard to do is false).

I think you forgot the /s
C is like a Winchester Model 1897, not saying that it is a bad gun, it is what we call it the good ol' Trench gun, as it ran over the war tunnels of the Great War, and it still packs a punch today, but it only has a half-cock for some level of safety, and there is no trigger disconnector either, so you can easily slam fire it. So it is way too easy to shoot yourself in the foot.
Nowadays we like going for a AKM/AK74, for something maybe more complicated but far more reliable at the same time. That's why we want Rust.

 [vote?id=50554877&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50554877]
Jon_Lowtek [user?id=Jon_Lowtek] 12 hours ago [item?id=50554877] | root [#50551749] | parent [#50553691] | next [#50550084] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

HTTP1.1 has a fundamental flaw related to request smuggling. Fixing specific variants of that problem is a game of whack a mole, as issues arise most often from larger server composition, not individual parts. Fixing the protocol flaw requires a major version increment and a phase out of the old one. Today the hesitation to do that gives us a mitigation option, but in general an http downgrade is a bad thing.
There are also features on top of http2 that a downgrade may break: gRPC and Server Side Events for example. The later is compatible with HTTP1.1, but could fail due to connection limits, as multiplexing isn't only about speed. Some web apps may handle that so gracefully, it turns into a performance thing again, but others could halt and catch fire.

reply [reply?id=50554877&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350554877]

xmcp123 [user?id=xmcp123] 13 hours ago [item?id=50553893] | parent [#50550101] | next [#50552649] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think the problem is that while what you say is true, AI is also capable of generating the special conditions required. And if it can’t do so reliably, it can sit there hammering the server for as long as it takes until the conditions are met - as an example hitting a bunch of load balanced servers until 2 requests hit the same workhorse sequentially.
It is completely realistic that we start seeing a lot of bugs a human can’t (reasonably) exploit, but AI can.

Well the fix for this seemingly already landed in the repo last week: https://github.com/nginx/nginx/commit/e27f5fa84fe6e98505e70b... [https://github.com/nginx/nginx/commit/e27f5fa84fe6e98505e70bc543b748747dad0a3b]
Given how common it's become to constantly scan popular repos for n-days, I expect someone will have already picked it up and started work on an exploit. The workaround for this (disabling HTTP/2) is also comparatively easy to what we had with PHP2Shell just a few weeks back, so I think it makes sense to release this.

 [vote?id=50548836&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374#50548836]
Twirrim [user?id=Twirrim] 20 hours ago [item?id=50548836] | root [#50547619] | parent [#50548030] | next [#50554555] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Even the US federal government (CISA and NSA) have been strongly advising companies to transition to memory safe languages for a couple of years now. https://www.nsa.gov/Press-Room/Press-Releases-Statements/Pre... [https://www.nsa.gov/Press-Room/Press-Releases-Statements/Press-Release-View/article/3608324/us-and-international-partners-issue-recommendations-to-secure-software-products/] (one of the goals of such advisories is it acts as a signal to the industry that in several years this will become a mandatory requirement around purchasing decisions)
We've literally decades of evidence that we cannot write software securely in languages that aren't memory safe, for all sorts of reasons (and it's wrong to look at it as a developer competence issue, even though sometimes that's going to be the case, even extremely experienced and highly competent engineers write memory safety bugs in critical code we rely on day in day out.). Not leaving yourself open to such problems is an entirely logical conclusion. We know a large percentage of vulnerabilities in software are caused by it[1]
Pick a language that doesn't have these risk factors, or pick an enforceable subset of one that makes such impossible.
"Insanity is doing the same thing over and over and expecting different results."
[1] https://www.chromium.org/Home/chromium-security/memory-safet... [https://www.chromium.org/Home/chromium-security/memory-safety/] 70% of Chrome vulnerabilities caused by it.
Similar stories for Microsoft products https://github.com/microsoft/MSRC-Security-Research/blob/mas... [https://github.com/microsoft/MSRC-Security-Research/blob/master/presentations/2019_02_BlueHatIL/2019_01%20-%20BlueHatIL%20-%20Trends%2C%20challenge%2C%20and%20shifts%20in%20software%20vulnerability%20mitigation.pdf]
and Mozilla: https://hacks.mozilla.org/2019/02/rewriting-a-browser-compon... [https://hacks.mozilla.org/2019/02/rewriting-a-browser-component-in-rust/].
Curl is at 40%, and dropping because Daniel Stenberg has provided various helpers that avoid it (and I believe they've now migrated almost everything over to them) https://daniel.haxx.se/blog/2023/12/13/making-it-harder-to-d... [https://daniel.haxx.se/blog/2023/12/13/making-it-harder-to-do-wrong/], so there are definitely ways to do it, we just... don't.

reply [reply?id=50548836&goto=item%3Fid%3D50547374%2350548836]

brynet [user?id=brynet] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553303] | prev [#50553325] | next [#50552805] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Nginx used to be in the OpenBSD base system between 2012-2015.
12 years on, OpenBSD's port of nginx still maintains its default chroot(2) patch.
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rnagy/nginx_chroot_patch/m... [https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rnagy/nginx_chroot_patch/master/nginx-1.30.0-chroot.patch]
I recommend reading Reyk Flöter's AsianBSDcon 2015 paper about nginx and the thing that replaced it in base.
https://www.openbsd.org/papers/httpd-asiabsdcon2015.pdf [https://www.openbsd.org/papers/httpd-asiabsdcon2015.pdf]

TZubiri [user?id=TZubiri] 18 hours ago [item?id=50550143] | prev [#50552805] | next [#50547566] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Hey this is TZubiri, the guy that comments that you should uninstall the dep whenever there is an post about a CVSS 9+ vuln.
Today we are going to take a look at how to avoid this vuln by not having Nginx and not using HTTP/2 (for most use cases).
We will assume that you are using Nginx for any of the following: Serving files or serving dynamic website content through some integration with a programming language.
We will be using python, but you can use any programming language that has a native integration with sockets through it's C interface (literally every language:
Step 1: import socket # import the socket library from glibc or glibc.
Step 2: socket.socket() # socket.socket()
Step 3: socket.bind((PUBLIC_ADDR,80)) # bind before listening obviously. Use a local address if you are below the IPoverty4 line.
Step 4: conn,addr = socket.accept() # this creates a connection, wrap it in a While True and a try catch for production. Sprinkle in some import threading to support multiple concurrent requests.
Step 4: req = conn.recv(4096) # split on b"\r\n\r\n" to separate headers from body, read HTTP RFCs for the rest. Feel free to reject HTTP/2 requests, they'll probably just resend as HTTP/1.1
Step 5: #Fill in the details, most likely if you are using nginx, you will end up connecting two sockets with pipe, or just storing and forwarding to another tcp endpoint. Again take a look at the HTTP RFCs and the glibc socket and stdio manuals.
Thank you for reading my tutorial, and best of lucks.

EGreg [user?id=EGreg] 17 hours ago [item?id=50551447] | prev [#50553517] | next [#50548576] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

If you are running a PHP site, you don't need an external webserver anymore, like Apache, NGINX or Caddy.
This webserver is not vulnerable to PHP2Shell nor this TLS issue, because it's completely userland PHP:
https://github.com/Qbix/webserver [https://github.com/Qbix/webserver]
It's now been battle-tested in production, and also supports websockets, etc.
(However, it doesn't support sendfile, because PHP doesn't have support for that, so it's not as fast as nginx for sending static files. This shouldn't make a difference, though, if you use a CDN in front.)
